The Visuals: You will eventually design your detailed bedroom, kitchen, and all the
objects (kettle, stove, cups) in a 3D modeling program like Blender.
1.
The Physics: You will import those 3D models into Gazebo. Gazebo adds real-
world physics like gravity, friction, and collisions. If your robot drops a cup, it will
actually fall and hit the floor!
2.
The Sensors: You will attach virtual cameras to your 3D robot inside Gazebo.
When you want the robot to "look at all the objects" as part of the sequence, it will
actually use Gazebo to see the 3D world through those virtual cameras.
3.
The Puppet Master: Your Python script (tea_robot.py) will talk directly to
Gazebo in the background. When your Python code says "drive to the kitchen,"
the 3D robot inside the Gazebo window will physically roll into the kitchen.

Ah, I know exactly what this is! That OGRE EXCEPTION error is a very common crash
when trying to open intense 3D graphics inside Windows Subsystem for Linux (WSL).
Because Ubuntu is running inside your Windows machine as a virtual system, it
occasionally struggles to directly access your Windows graphics card (GPU) to draw
complex 3D lighting and textures. When Gazebo tries to use a specific graphics feature
that WSL can't translate, it immediately crashes.
Fortunately, there is a very simple fix. We just need to tell Ubuntu to use a safe,
software-based rendering mode instead of trying to force hardware graphics.
🛠  Step 1: Force Software Rendering
Copy and paste this exact command into your terminal and press Enter:
(Note: This command won't print anything to the screen, but it silently changes the
graphics settings for this specific window.)
🌍  Step 2: Try Launching Gazebo Again
export LIBGL_ALWAYS_SOFTWARE=1

🔥  Why Your Laptop is Heating Up
When we opened Gazebo, it launched a full 3D physics simulation. Because WSL was
crashing earlier, we used a trick to force it into "Software Rendering" mode. This means
your computer's CPU is doing all the heavy lifting to draw that 3D world instead of using
your graphics card.
Running a 3D simulator entirely on a CPU is incredibly intense work for a laptop, so the
fans are kicking into overdrive to keep it cool. It is completely normal for robotics
simulation!
❌  Why the Command Failed
You typed wsl --shutdown inside your Ubuntu terminal.
wsl is a Windows command used to control Linux.
Because you are typing it inside Linux, Ubuntu has no idea what you are talking
about!

The reason Gazebo doesn't show up in your desktop or start menu is because you are
running Ubuntu inside WSL (Windows Subsystem for Linux).
In WSL, Linux software is installed directly inside your command-line environment, so
Windows doesn't automatically create desktop icons or menu shortcuts for Linux 3D
applications.
You launch Gazebo directly from your Ubuntu terminal prompt using commands!
🚀  How to Launch Gazebo
Whenever you want to start Gazebo, open your Ubuntu terminal (argus@Sneha) and
follow these steps:
Step 1: Set the GPU Compatibility Flag (To Prevent Crashing)
Before opening 3D graphics in WSL, run this command so your laptop doesn't crash:
export LIBGL_ALWAYS_SOFTWARE=1

🏗  Step 2: Paste the Robot Blueprint
Once the blank editor opens, right-click to paste this basic skeleton code. This creates
the main body (base_link) and attaches your three required sensors using joints:
nano ~/argus_ws/src/argus_tasks/urdf/argus_robot.urdf
<?xml version="1.0"?>
<robotname="argus_robot">
<!-- BASE LINK (The main chassis of your robot) -->
<linkname="base_link">
<visual>
<geometry>
<boxsize="0.5 0.5 0.2"/>
</geometry>
<materialname="blue">
<colorrgba="0 0 1 1"/>
</material>
</visual>
</link>
<!-- 360 LiDAR SENSOR -->
<linkname="lidar_link">
<visual>
<geometry>
<cylinderradius="0.05"length="0.1"/>
</geometry>
<materialname="red">
<colorrgba="1 0 0 1"/>
</material>
</visual>
</link>
<jointname="lidar_joint"type="fixed">
<parentlink="base_link"/>
<childlink="lidar_link"/>
<originxyz="0 0 0.15"rpy="0 0 0"/>
</joint>

While I cannot generate a direct picture file for you as an AI, I can give you the exact
conceptual blueprint and measurements. Since this robot is an autonomous escort
designed to operate in dynamic environments and follow a walking human at a fixed 1.5-
meter safety offset, it needs to be stable, low to the ground, and have unobstructed
views for its sensors.
Here is the recommended physical design for the ARGUS robot:
🚙  1. The Chassis (Main Body)
The robot needs to be large enough to hold a battery and a computer, but compact
enough to weave around obstacles.
🛞  2. The Wheels (Differential Drive)
To turn sharply and navigate unstructured spaces, it will use two large drive wheels on
the sides and a small caster wheel (like a shopping cart wheel) in the back for balance.
👁  3. The Sensors & Exact Placements
Shape: Rectangular box (like a sturdy industrial rover).
Length: 0.4 meters (40 cm).
Width: 0.3 meters (30 cm).
Height: 0.2 meters (20 cm).
Drive Wheel Radius: 0.1 meters (10 cm) to provide enough ground clearance to
roll over small bumps or cables.
Drive Wheel Width: 0.05 meters (5 cm) for good traction.
Placement: Mounted directly on the left and right sides of the chassis, slightly
towards the front.

Every sensor must be placed strategically so they do not block each other's view.

Do these measurements sound perfect for your vision of the robot, or would you prefer

360° LiDAR Scanner:
Purpose: To create the geometric map and detect structural obstacles all
the way around the robot.
Placement: Mounted on a small pedestal in the dead center of the roof. It
must be the highest point on the robot so the cameras don't block its laser
scans.
Forward RGB-D Camera:
Purpose: To run the YOLO vision pipeline, classify objects in front of it, and
track the human it is escorting.
Placement: Mounted on the top-front edge of the chassis, angled straight
ahead.
Top-Facing Anti-Crush Camera:
Purpose: To look straight up at the ceiling to detect falling debris.
Placement: Mounted flat on the back-half of the roof, pointing 90 degrees
straight up into the air.

🎨  The ARGUS Concept: Sleek, Hubless, and Agile Escort
Instead of a bulky industrial box, let’s design a modern robot inspired by aquatic
movement and hubless wheel designs, allowing it to "move flexibly."
Visual Design Description:
Chassis (The Body): Imagine a segmented, manta-ray-shaped fuselage. It is
composed of overlapping, curved panels of matte black and deep cobalt blue

--- Page 229 ---
📊  ASCII Diagram of the Modern "Manta-Hubless" Design
composites. This segmented design makes the robot look like it could flex or
articulate, rather than being a static block[cite: 1].
The Wheels (Hubless "Flexible Movement" Style):
We will replace solid wheels with a central axle with large, hubless, center-
less traction rings. These are sleek, open rings with no visible center
mechanism, looking incredibly futuristic and modern.
They are mounted on stylized, arched suspension arms on the sides, angled
slightly to make the robot look like it is leaning forward, ready to move
agilely around obstacles.
The Sensors (Seamless Integration):
360° LiDAR: Instead of being on a pole, it is integrated into a sleek, central
translucent dome on the roof, glowing with a soft red light[cite: 1].
Forward RGB-D Camera: This is hidden behind a dark, smoked transparent
front cowl (visor) that matches the segmented body aesthetic[cite: 1].
Top-Facing Anti-Crush Camera: This is an omni-directional "eye" camera
integrated seamlessly into the top back of the fuselage[cite: 1].
Rear Stability: A small, transparent sphere (omni-directional caster wheel) is
integrated under the rear fuselage for balance, looking minimalist.

<?xml version="1.0"?>

<!-- =============================== -->
<materialname="matte_black"><colorrgba="0.05 0.05 0.05 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="matte_blue"><colorrgba="0.1 0.2 0.5 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="glowing_blue"><colorrgba="0.0 0.8 1.0 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="glowing_red"><colorrgba="0.9 0.1 0.1 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="smoked_glass"><colorrgba="0.1 0.1 0.1 0.7"/></material>
<materialname="metal"><colorrgba="0.6 0.6 0.6 1.0"/></material>
<!-- =============================== -->
<!-- BASE LINK (Segmentation for Flexibility) -->

🛠  Step 1: Add Physics & Collision to the URDF
For Gazebo to simulate a robot instead of letting it fall through the floor, the URDF
needs collision boundaries and inertial physics properties.
Open your URDF file in your terminal:
Replace the contents with this Gazebo-ready URDF (which adds collision boxes, mass,
and wheel joints so it can move on the Gazebo floor):
PDF
nano ~/argus_ws/src/argus_tasks/urdf/argus_robot_modern.urdf
<?xml version="1.0"?>
<robotname="argus_robot_modern">
<!-- MATERIALS -->
<materialname="matte_black"><colorrgba="0.05 0.05 0.05 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="matte_blue"><colorrgba="0.1 0.2 0.5 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="glowing_blue"><colorrgba="0.0 0.8 1.0 1.0"/></material>
<materialname="glowing_red"><colorrgba="0.9 0.1 0.1 1.0"/></material>
<!-- BASE LINK / CHASSIS -->
<linkname="base_link">
<visual>
<geometry><boxsize="0.45 0.35 0.15"/></geometry>
<materialname="matte_blue"/>
</visual>
<collision>
<geometry><boxsize="0.45 0.35 0.15"/></geometry>
</collision>
<inertial>
<massvalue="5.0"/>
<originxyz="0 0 0"rpy="0 0 0"/>
<inertiaixx="0.06"ixy="0"ixz="0"iyy="0.09"iyz="0"izz="0.12"/>Gemini may display inaccurate info, including about people, so double-check its responses. This content was created by

No worries at all! This happens frequently when spawning URDF models in Gazebo
Fortress.
Usually, ros_gz_sim doesn't know how to expand the tilde (~) symbol in file paths, or
the simulation is paused in the background.
Let's fix this in 3 quick checks:
1️⃣  Check if Gazebo is Paused
Look at the bottom left corner of your Gazebo window. If there is a Play button ( ▶ ),
click it! Sometimes Gazebo holds spawned objects in a paused state until you unpause
physics.
2️⃣  Spawn using the Full Explicit Path
In Terminal 2, hit Ctrl + C to clear the prompt, and run the command using the full
absolute path /home/argus/... instead of ~/...:
3️⃣  What Terminal 2 Should Say

There is a known quirk when working with ROS 2 and Gazebo Sim (Fortress or later). The
create executable from the ros_gz_sim package can indeed spawn URDF files
from disk, but Gazebo has a very strict requirement: it plainly ignores links that are
missing physics parameters.
Even though we fixed the lidar_link by adding the <inertial> tags, Gazebo
often completely fails to render any part of a URDF file directly using the -file
argument if it cannot perfectly translate all of the XML tags on the fly.
To fix this reliably, we must use the standard ROS 2 approach for spawning URDFs into
Gazebo: passing the URDF string through a ROS parameter using the Robot State
Publisher.
Let's do this the correct, industry-standard way.
🧹  Step 1: Clean Slate
🤖  Step 2: The Bulletproof Spawn Method (Terminal 2)

invisibility issue. Instead, we will use a small inline Python command to read your URDF

Open Terminal 1 and start a fresh Gazebo world:2.

ros2 run ros_gz_sim create -string "$(cat /home/argus/argus_ws/src/argus_task

🛑  The Difference Between Concept Art and 3D Simulation
🏗  How We Bridge the Gap (The Engineering Secret)
To get a robot that looks exactly like your modern concept art into Gazebo, you cannot
just use raw URDF code. You have to build the robot's outer shell in a 3D modeling
software like Blender or SolidWorks, export it as a 3D mesh file (like an .stl or .dae
file), and then link that 3D mesh to your URDF code.
However, real robotics engineers always start exactly where you are right now!
AI Image Generation (The Concept): The highly detailed, modern image you see
with the glowing rings and sleek curves is a 2D concept image. As an AI, I can
generate inspirational 2D pictures to show you what the final product could look
like, but I cannot generate actual 3D CAD files.
1.
URDF Primitives (The Reality): Gazebo is a hardcore physics engine. When we
write a URDF file using code, we are limited to basic geometric primitives: 
<box>, <cylinder>, and <sphere>. That is why your Gazebo robot looks like
it is made out of basic Lego bricks.
2.

Would you like to learn how to add open-source 3D mesh files (.stl) to your URDF, or

how to operate ubuntu, gazebo, vm through VS?
Transitioning from basic terminal editors like nano to a professional Integrated
Development Environment (IDE) like Visual Studio Code (VS Code) is the perfect next
step! It is exactly how professional robotics engineers manage complex ROS 2 and
Gazebo projects.
Because your Ubuntu Linux environment is running inside Windows via WSL, you can
use your Windows VS Code application to write code, while all the heavy lifting,
compilation, and ROS 2 execution happens seamlessly in Ubuntu.
Here is the exact setup to bridge your Windows VS Code with your Ubuntu workspace:
🔌  Step 1: Install the WSL Extension in VS Code
First, we need to give your Windows VS Code the ability to reach inside your Linux
system.
Open Visual Studio Code on your Windows desktop (not in WSL).1.
Click on the Extensions icon on the left sidebar (or press Ctrl+Shift+X).2.

⚠  A Crucial Warning for ROS 2 Engineers
As a robotics engineer, I have to be completely candid with you about a major technical
hurdle.
While you can store your project on the Windows E: drive, ROS 2 strongly dislikes
being built on Windows drives. When we try to compile the argus_tasks package
later, the ROS 2 compiler (colcon) tries to create Linux-specific shortcuts (called
symlinks). Because the E: drive is formatted for Windows (NTFS), the compiler will
crash and give you massive error walls.
The Industry Standard Solution:
Keep your working code (the argus_ws folder) inside the Linux home directory
(~/argus_ws) so it compiles perfectly. Then, use your E:\Internship\Project

Windows storage (E:\ drive formatted as NTFS) blocks Linux symlinks unless

--- Page 276 ---
Linux IS connected to your E: drive! Linux can see and open every single file on your 
E: drive anytime you want.
In Linux, your Windows drives live under the /mnt/ (mount) folder:
❓  So Why Don't We Put the ROS 2 Code Folder Directly on E:?
There are 2 main technical reasons why every robotics engineer keeps their active
workspace inside the Linux home folder (~/argus_ws) instead of E:\:
1. ⚡  Speed (Performance)
WSL2 is a real Linux system running alongside Windows.
2. 🛠  Linux Shortcuts ("Symlinks")
When you build a ROS 2 Python package using colcon build, ROS 2 creates special
Linux file shortcuts called symlinks.
💡  The Perfect Solution We Are Using
We get the best of both worlds:
C:\ drive in Windows = /mnt/c/ in Linux
E:\ drive in Windows = /mnt/e/ in Linux
When files are inside ~/argus_ws (Linux storage), reading and writing files
happens instantly.
When files are inside E:\ (Windows storage), Linux has to send a request to
Windows every time it wants to read or compile a file. This makes building code 
up to 10 times slower!
Linux native storage (~/) supports these shortcuts perfectly.
Windows drives (E:\, formatted as NTFS) do not handle Linux symlinks properly,
which causes colcon build to crash with error messages.
~/argus_ws (Inside Linux): This is where ROS 2 compiles, runs Gazebo, and
executes Python code at full speed with zero build errors.

Exactly! You are keeping the functional code folder (argus_ws) directly in your Ubuntu
home directory, which will look like ~/argus_ws or /home/argus/argus_ws.
Here is why this is the standard practice:
By keeping the code in the home directory, you get lightning-fast compilation, and we
simply use your E:\Internship\Project folder as a safe, connected storage unit for
your screenshots, PDF reports, and design files.

Maximum Performance: WSL2 file systems are significantly faster when
accessing their native Linux storage.
Avoiding Build Hangs: Compiling ROS 2 code with colcon build inside a
mounted Windows drive (like your E: drive at /mnt/e/) is known to be much
slower and can cause the build process to freeze or hang up completely.

🚀  Next Step: Create argus_bringup.launch.py
Now that your workspace lives safely in ~/argus_ws, let's permanently save your
startup sequence into a ROS 2 Launch File.
This file will allow you to start Gazebo, load the empty world, and spawn the ARGUS
robot automatically with a single command!
1. Open the file in VS Code
In your VS Code file explorer on the left, navigate to:
src/argus_tasks/launch/argus_bringup.launch.py
2. Paste this Python code into argus_bringup.launch.py:
cp -r /mnt/e/Internship/Project/argus_ws ~/
import os
from ament_index_python.packages import get_package_share_directory
from launch import LaunchDescription
from launch.actions import ExecuteProcess, IncludeLaunchDescription
from launch.launch_description_sources import PythonLaunchDescriptionSource
from launch_ros.actions import Node

Would you like to update your setup.py next so that ROS 2 knows how to install and

defgenerate_launch_description():
# 1. Path to your URDF robot model file
    pkg_argus = get_package_share_directory('argus_tasks')
    urdf_file = os.path.join(pkg_argus, 'urdf', 'argus_robot_modern.urdf')
# Read the URDF file contents
withopen(urdf_file, 'r') as infp:
        robot_desc = infp.read()
# 2. Launch Gazebo Simulation World (using ros_gz_sim)
    gz_sim = IncludeLaunchDescription(
        PythonLaunchDescriptionSource(
            os.path.join(
                get_package_share_directory('ros_gz_sim'),
'launch',
'gz_sim.launch.py'
            )
        ),
        launch_arguments={'gz_args': '-r empty.sdf'}.items()
    )
# 3. Spawn the ARGUS Robot into Gazebo
    spawn_robot = Node(
        package='ros_gz_sim',
        executable='create',
        arguments=[
'-string', robot_desc,
'-name', 'argus_robot',
'-z', '0.5'
        ],
        output='screen'
    )
return LaunchDescription([
        gz_sim,
        spawn_robot
    ])

--- Page 280 ---
see where is not any folder
Don't worry—your folder isn't missing!
The reason Windows File Explorer isn't showing argus_ws in your search results is
because Windows Search only looks through your Windows drives (C:\ and E:\).
It does not automatically index files inside the hidden Ubuntu Linux (WSL) virtual disk.
Your argus_ws folder is safe and sound inside Linux (/home/argus/argus_ws).
📂  How to Open Your Linux Folders in Windows File Explorer
If you ever want to see your Linux files in Windows File Explorer:
Option 1: Use the Address Bar (Fastest)
Open Windows File Explorer.1.
Click on the address bar at the top, paste this exact path, and press Enter:2.
